OpenRemote bulk alarm deletion by non-human / service account (stolen-credential scope escalation) AIActions on Objectives Low
Hunts the scenario where a compromised service or bot account — which needs only authentication, not admin — is used to bulk-delete alarms via the removeAlarms IDOR. Flags DELETE /api/{realm}/alarm activity from accounts matching non-interactive/service naming, which should rarely if ever perform destructive alarm operations. Rationale: Combines the article's DELETE /api/{realm}/alarm route with a service-account name filter to catch stolen-credential abuse of the IDOR, since the vuln requires only authentication and service accounts performing bulk alarm deletes is anomalous. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-h3m5-97jq-qjrf
ATT&CKT1078 T1485
Data sourcesWeb.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A legitimate integration service account that manages alarm lifecycle by design
  • Automation account performing scheduled alarm cleanup
  • Naming-convention overlap where a human account starts with 'api'
  • Monitoring bot that creates and deletes test alarms
  • Service account operating within a single, authorised realm
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): Verify whether the service account is authorised to delete alarms and whether the targeted realm(s) match its intended scope; multi-realm deletions from one service identity indicate credential abuse. Step 2 (contain): Rotate the service account's credentials/API keys, restrict its role, and patch to >= 1.24.2. Step 3 (hunt): Trace where the service credential was used from (source IPs, other endpoints hit) to find the initial compromise and any additional cross-tenant impact.

Archive (TAR/ZIP) upload to Langflow BaseFileComponent file API (CVE-2026-55447 delivery) AIDelivery Medium
Hunts POST uploads of tar/zip archives to Langflow's file-ingestion endpoints (/api/v1/files/upload, /api/v2/files) that feed BaseFileComponent-derived nodes (Read File, Docling, Unstructured). This is the delivery vector for the symlink-TAR arbitrary-file-read in CVE-2026-55447, where a crafted archive is uploaded to a RAG ingestion flow. Rationale: Uses the article's named ingestion path (BaseFileComponent file upload) plus archive extensions; the endpoint strings /api/v1/files/upload and /api/v2/files are Langflow-specific upload routes that feed the vulnerable nodes. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/pypi/langflow/CVE-2026-55447/ • https://www.ibm.com/support/pages/security-bulletin-path-traversal-vulnerability-file-processing-components-allows-unauthorized-file-system-access-and-potential-remote-code-execution
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate users uploading zipped document bundles into a RAG ingestion flow
  • Backup/export archives POSTed to the files API by an integration
  • Automated data-loader jobs bulk-uploading tar-packaged corpora
  • Security scanners / DAST tools probing the upload endpoint
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the uploaded archive from Langflow storage and inspect TAR members with `tar -tvf` — flag any entry whose type is symlink/hardlink or whose linkname is an absolute path (/etc, /root, /.ssh, secret_key). 2) Contain: if malicious members present, quarantine the archive, revoke the uploader's session/API token, and confirm Langflow is patched to >=1.9.2. 3) Hunt: pivot to the Langflow host and check whether the worker process subsequently read credential files (UC 'credential access') or made outbound connections (UC 'exfiltration').
ImWebSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where HttpMethod =~ "POST"
| where Url has_any ("/api/v1/files/upload", "/api/v2/files", "/api/v1/files/")
| where FileName has_any (".tar", ".tgz", ".tar.gz", ".zip") or HttpResponseHeaderContentType has_any ("tar", "zip", "octet-stream")
| project TimeGenerated, SrcIpAddr, SrcUsername, DstHostname, DstIpAddr, Url, HttpMethod, FileName, HttpUserAgent = HttpRequestHeaderUserAgent, HttpStatusCode
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Langflow worker reads JWT secret_key / OS credential files via symlink-TAR (CVE-2026-55447) AIActions on Objectives High
Detects the Langflow Python worker (python/uvicorn/gunicorn/langflow) opening sensitive files outside its working tree — the JWT secret_key file, /etc/shadow, /etc/passwd, ~/.ssh, ~/.aws/credentials — which is the core arbitrary-file-read primitive of CVE-2026-55447 that leads to JWT forgery and RCE. Rationale: Directly targets the article's exploit: the worker reading Langflow's secret_key (JWT signing secret) plus the reviewer-supplied credential paths (/etc/shadow, ~/.ssh, ~/.aws/credentials). A RAG worker opening /etc/shadow or a secret_key outside its config context is near-zero in normal operation. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/pypi/langflow/CVE-2026-55447/ • https://www.ibm.com/support/pages/security-bulletin-path-traversal-vulnerability-file-processing-components-allows-unauthorized-file-system-access-and-potential-remote-code-execution
ATT&CKT1552.001 T1083
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Langflow legitimately reading its OWN secret_key from LANGFLOW_CONFIG_DIR at startup (scope to reads of paths outside the config dir)
  • An operator's interactive python shell on the same host reading ~/.ssh for git/ssh ops
  • Backup agent running under the same service account touching /etc or /root
  • Config-management (Ansible/Salt) python modules reading /etc/passwd
Suppression filter
| where not (FolderPath has "secret_key" and FolderPath has_any ("/.cache/langflow/", "/app/langflow/", "/root/.cache/langflow/"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the accessing PID is the Langflow web/worker process (not an admin shell) and that the accessed path is OUTSIDE Langflow's configured secret dir; check the ingested vector store for the leaked file contents. 2) Contain: rotate the Langflow secret_key (invalidates forged JWTs), force logout of all sessions, rotate any credentials the file exposed (SSH keys, AWS keys), and patch to >=1.9.2. 3) Hunt: search for anomalous JWT-authenticated flow creations (esp. Python Interpreter nodes) and for the RCE/exfil UCs on this host.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName matches regex @"(?i)^(python[0-9.]*|uvicorn|gunicorn|langflow)$"
| where FolderPath has_any ("/etc/shadow", "/etc/passwd", "/.ssh/", "/.aws/credentials", "secret_key", "/root/")
     or FileName in~ ("shadow", "passwd", "id_rsa", "credentials", "secret_key")
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, ActionType, FolderPath, FileName
| order by Timestamp desc

| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ActingProcessName matches regex @"(?i)(python[0-9.]*|uvicorn|gunicorn|langflow)$"
| where TargetFilePath has_any ("/etc/shadow", "/etc/passwd", "/.ssh/", "/.aws/credentials", "secret_key", "/root/")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, EventType, TargetFilePath, TargetFileName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Langflow Python worker spawns shell / network LOLBin (CVE-2026-55447 Python Interpreter node RCE) AIInstallation High
Detects the Langflow worker process (python/uvicorn/gunicorn/langflow) spawning an interactive shell or network tool (sh, bash, curl, wget, nc, socat). After JWT forgery, the CVE-2026-55447 chain achieves RCE via a Python Interpreter node whose payload shells out — a web/RAG worker parenting /bin/sh is highly anomalous. Rationale: Grounded in the article's stated RCE step — a Python Interpreter node running attacker code. A Langflow web/worker process (python/uvicorn/gunicorn) is not expected to fork shells or curl/nc; parent+child pairing turns generic execution into intent. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/pypi/langflow/CVE-2026-55447/ • https://gbhackers.com/langflow-rce-flaw/
ATT&CKT1059.006 T1059.004
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Langflow components that legitimately shell out (e.g. a shell-command tool node an operator deliberately configured)
  • Container entrypoint/health-check scripts where uvicorn is PID1 and runs `sh -c`
  • python-based subprocess calls in custom components (e.g. calling `curl` for a webhook)
  • CI/agent tasks running under the same service account
Suppression filter
| where not (ProcessCommandLine has_any ("/healthcheck", "docker-entrypoint", "livenessProbe"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: capture the child process command line and the parent Langflow PID; determine if a Python Interpreter / shell-command node was recently added to a flow. 2) Contain: kill the child process tree, isolate the host, rotate the Langflow secret_key and any exposed credentials, and block outbound to any C2 the child contacted. 3) Hunt: enumerate all flows for Python Interpreter nodes with suspicious code, review who created/edited flows via the API (forged-JWT accounts), and check for persistence (cron, systemd, SSH authorized_keys) dropped by the child.

Correlates a Langflow worker reading a sensitive credential/secret file with an outbound public network connection from the same worker within 5 minutes. Catches post-exploitation exfiltration of the stolen JWT secret_key or OS credentials in the CVE-2026-55447 chain. Rationale: Chains the article's two decisive artifacts — the worker reading secret_key/OS credentials and then egressing — inside a tight 5-minute window, which the reviewer flagged as the missing post-exploitation context. Langflow worker creates symlink in temp dir during TAR extraction (CVE-2026-55447 traversal setup) AIExploitation Medium
Detects the Langflow worker creating symbolic links / files in /tmp or /var/tmp during BaseFileComponent TAR extraction — the actual _unpack_bundle attack step where a malicious tar's symlink member is written to disk pointing at an out-of-tree credential path before process_files() reads the target. Rationale: Implements the reviewer's traversal-setup hypothesis using the article's _unpack_bundle mechanism: the worker writes tar members into a temp extraction dir; auditd symlink syscalls are the accurate telemetry, with DeviceFileEvents/FileWritten as best-effort where symlink syscall logging is absent. Names/'..' filter narrows to credential-targeting members. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/pypi/langflow/CVE-2026-55447/ • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-ccv6-r384-xp75

  • Antivirus or backup software staging files in /var/tmp

Response runbook

Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] Langflow IDOR exploitation via repeated POST /api/v1/responses (CVE-2026-55255) · [LLM] Langflow recon-to-exploit chain: flow enumeration then /api/v1/responses from same source
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Langflow flow-ID harvesting via burst GET /api/v1/flows enumeration AIReconnaissance Medium
Hunts a single client rapidly enumerating the Langflow flow API (/api/v1/flows*) to collect valid flow UUIDs — the reconnaissance step that precedes CVE-2026-55255 IDOR exploitation. Article-specific because it keys on the exact Langflow flow-listing/read API paths the attacker walks before abusing /api/v1/responses. Rationale: Uses the exact Langflow flow API path family (/api/v1/flows*) that the corroborated Sysdig/Mallory writeup shows the attacker enumerating to collect valid UUIDs before IDOR replay; volumetric per-source threshold catches harvesting that individual requests wouldn't. Cross-checked against: • https://www.sysdig.com/blog/understanding-langflow-cve-2026-55255-and-why-higher-cvss-vulnerabilities-arent-always-the-most-exploited • https://advisories.gitlab.com/pypi/langflow/CVE-2026-55255/ • https://www.mallory.ai/stories/019ef684-a9a6-79cd-b1c7-47a36cffec6e
ATT&CKT1526 T1190
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Power user/admin opening many flows in the Langflow UI (each open = a flow GET)
  • CI/CD or IaC automation that lists and validates flows on every deploy
  • Monitoring/health-check job polling the flows API on a schedule
  • A dashboard or embedded app auto-refreshing the flow list frequently
  • Bulk import/export of flows by a legitimate operator
Suppression filter
| where csUsername !in ("svc-langflow-ci","svc-monitoring") and cIP !in ("10.10.0.0")   // replace with your automation accounts / internal orchestration IPs
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Confirm the cIP/csUsername is not a known internal automation or admin. Check whether the flow API reads span UUIDs the principal does not own (join against Langflow flow-ownership export if available). 2) CONTAIN: If unowned flows were read, revoke the offending Langflow API key (x-api-key) and block the source IP at the WAF/reverse proxy; force key rotation. 3) HUNT: Pivot on the same cIP/key to POST /api/v1/responses and /api/v1/run* activity (UC2/UC3) and to any /api/v1/validate/code calls (chained RCE, CVE-2025-3248) within the following minutes.
W3CIISLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where csUriStem startswith "/api/v1/flows"
| summarize RequestCount = count(), DistinctFlowPaths = dcount(csUriStem), Methods = make_set(csMethod), Statuses = make_set(scStatus), FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen = max(TimeGenerated) by cIP, csUsername, sSiteName
| where RequestCount >= 30 or DistinctFlowPaths >= 15   // burst of flow-API reads = UUID harvesting; tune per legit UI usage
| order by RequestCount desc

Langflow IDOR exploitation via repeated POST /api/v1/responses (CVE-2026-55255) AIExploitation Medium
Hunts a single client (IP or API key) invoking the vulnerable Langflow /api/v1/responses endpoint many times — the CVE-2026-55255 IDOR that executes another user's flow by supplying its UUID. Article-specific: the endpoint, method, and the 200/404 outcome pattern come straight from the advisory PoC and the fix's 404-oracle note. Rationale: Keys on the exact vulnerable route (POST /api/v1/responses) named in the advisory and its 200-vs-404 outcome oracle (the fix explicitly changed cross-user lookups to 404); a per-principal repetition threshold separates IDOR abuse from a single legitimate call. Cross-checked against: • https://www.sysdig.com/blog/understanding-langflow-cve-2026-55255-and-why-higher-cvss-vulnerabilities-arent-always-the-most-exploited • https://advisories.gitlab.com/pypi/langflow/CVE-2026-55255/
ATT&CKT1190 T1550.001
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A legitimate OpenAI-compatible client/integration that heavily uses the /api/v1/responses shim
  • Chatbot or app frontend making many flow-execution calls for real users
  • Load/perf testing against the responses endpoint
  • A batch job that executes the same owned flow repeatedly
  • Automated regression tests replaying responses calls after a deploy
Suppression filter
| where csUsername !in ("svc-langflow-app","svc-chatbot") and cIP !in ("10.20.0.0")   // replace with legitimate integration accounts / app-tier IPs
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Determine whether the 200 responses correspond to flows the calling principal actually owns (correlate the x-api-key/user to flow ownership). A mix of 404 (cross-user misses) then 200 strongly indicates IDOR success. 2) CONTAIN: Revoke the API key, block the source IP, and upgrade Langflow to >= 1.9.1 immediately if still < 1.9.1. 3) HUNT: Review flows executed for secret-leaking prompts (e.g., input 'leak api keys'), rotate any credentials referenced by the victim flows, and check for follow-on /api/v1/validate/code calls (chained RCE).
W3CIISLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where csUriStem =~ "/api/v1/responses"
| where csMethod == "POST"
| summarize InvocationCount = count(), StatusSet = make_set(scStatus), Success200 = countif(scStatus == 200), NotFound404 = countif(scStatus == 404), FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen = max(TimeGenerated) by cIP, csUsername, sSiteName
| where InvocationCount >= 10   // repeated IDOR-endpoint invocation from one principal; 404s = cross-user misses on patched hosts, 200s = hits
| order by InvocationCount desc

Langflow recon-to-exploit chain: flow enumeration then /api/v1/responses from same source AIExploitation High
High-fidelity correlation of the observed CVE-2026-55255 campaign: the same client first enumerates flows (/api/v1/flows*) to harvest UUIDs, then replays them into POST /api/v1/responses. The two-endpoint sequence from one source within a short window is far more specific than either signal alone. Rationale: Reproduces the exact two-step campaign chain documented by Sysdig/Mallory (query /api/v1/flows/ to collect UUIDs, then POST them to /api/v1/responses) as a same-source, recon-before-exploit correlation — the highest-fidelity, lowest-FP expression of this IDOR. Cross-checked against: • https://www.mallory.ai/stories/019ef684-a9a6-79cd-b1c7-47a36cffec6e • https://www.sysdig.com/blog/understanding-langflow-cve-2026-55255-and-why-higher-cvss-vulnerabilities-arent-always-the-most-exploited • https://advisories.gitlab.com/pypi/langflow/CVE-2026-55255/
ATT&CKT1190 T1213
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • An internal orchestration tool that lists flows and then executes owned flows in sequence
  • Admin post-deploy smoke test: enumerate flows, run one to verify
  • A workflow-scheduler service that discovers then triggers its own flows
  • CI pipeline that lists and then test-executes flows it created
Suppression filter
| where csUsername !in ("svc-langflow-orchestrator","svc-langflow-ci") and cIP !in ("10.20.0.0")   // sanctioned orchestration accounts / IPs
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: The recon+exploit sequence from one source is rarely benign — confirm the principal is not a sanctioned orchestrator, and check whether executed flow UUIDs belong to other users. 2) CONTAIN: Revoke the API key, block the IP, and patch to Langflow >= 1.9.1. 3) HUNT: Enumerate every flow UUID this source touched, notify those flow owners, rotate secrets/keys those flows reference, and look for exfil of flow outputs or subsequent /api/v1/validate/code (RCE) and /api/v2/workflow calls from the same source.
let recon = W3CIISLog
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
    | where csUriStem startswith "/api/v1/flows"

| join kind=inner recon on cIP
| where ExploitFirst >= ReconFirst   // reconnaissance preceded exploitation from the same source
| where ReconHits >= 5 and ExploitHits >= 1
| project cIP, csUsername, ReconFirst, ReconHits, ExploitFirst, ExploitHits, Success
| order by ExploitFirst desc

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] Craft CMS resource-js Host/X-Forwarded-Host poisoning (CVE-2026-55791) · [LLM] Craft CMS trustedHosts bypass via localhost/127.0.0.1 Host header from external IP
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code.
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] Blind SSRF: Craft web-server process fetching internal/cloud-metadata hosts
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] Craft CMS 1-click RCE: plugin-install POST riding a poisoned resource-js JS load
Craft CMS resource-js Host/X-Forwarded-Host poisoning (CVE-2026-55791) AIDelivery Medium
Hunts unauthenticated requests to Craft's /actions/app/resource-js endpoint where a single client cycles multiple distinct Host header values, the fingerprint of trustedHosts:['any'] abuse poisoning $baseUrl. Article-specific: only this endpoint reflects attacker-controlled content as application/javascript. Rationale: Anchored on the exact vulnerable URI 'app/resource-js' and the article's stated mechanism of poisoning the Host header to manipulate $baseUrl; dcount(Host) per client avoids needing the victim's domain (no placeholder) while flagging the header-rewriting pattern. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/composer/craftcms/cms/CVE-2026-55791/ • https://advisories.gitlab.com/composer/craftcms/cms/GHSA-95wr-3f2v-v2wh/
ATT&CKT1190 T1071.001
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate multi-domain Craft install serving several front-end hostnames from one CDN/proxy egress IP
  • Uptime/synthetic monitors probing the site with varied Host headers
  • Security scanners (Burp, Nuclei) run by the org's own pentest team
  • Shared NAT egress where many real users appear as one SrcIpAddr
  • CDN revalidation requests carrying origin-shield Host values
Suppression filter
| where DstHostname !in ("monitoring.internal")  // append known synthetic-monitor egress hosts after baselining
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull HostSamples for the flagged SrcIpAddr — confirm values are IP literals, internal names, or attacker domains rather than the site's real hostnames; check response status/size on those requests. 2) Contain: in config/general.php set trustedHosts to the explicit allowed host(s) and upgrade craftcms/cms to >=4.17.9 / >=5.9.15; block the source IP at the WAF. 3) Hunt: pivot to UC for outbound fetches from the web-server process and to admin plugin-install POSTs from the same window.
let lookback = 24h;
ImWebSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(lookback)
| where Url has "resource-js"
| extend HostHeader = tostring(HttpHostName)
| where isnotempty(HostHeader)
| summarize DistinctHosts = dcount(HostHeader), HostSamples = make_set(HostHeader, 10), Requests = count(), FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen = max(TimeGenerated) by SrcIpAddr, DstHostname
| where DistinctHosts > 3   // one client cycling >3 distinct Host values at resource-js = header fuzzing / baseUrl poisoning
| order by DistinctHosts desc

Craft CMS trustedHosts bypass via localhost/127.0.0.1 Host header from external IP AIDelivery Medium
Detects requests to Craft's resource-js endpoint where the Host or X-Forwarded-Host header claims to be localhost/127.0.0.1/::1 but the source IP is public — a direct attempt to defeat front-end Host validation against the trustedHosts:['any'] default. Single occurrence is high-fidelity. Rationale: Uses the exact vulnerable endpoint plus the concrete loopback literals attackers supply to spoof a trusted origin; pairing a loopback Host with a public source IP is behaviour no legitimate client produces, making it a low-FP alerting candidate. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/composer/craftcms/cms/CVE-2026-55791/ • https://advisories.gitlab.com/composer/craftcms/cms/GHSA-95wr-3f2v-v2wh/
ATT&CKT1190 T1199
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Internal health-check that wrongly egresses through a public IP while sending Host: localhost
  • Misconfigured reverse proxy forwarding a literal localhost Host upstream
  • Container-to-container probe traversing a public NAT
  • Pentest/scanner deliberately fuzzing Host headers
  • Load balancer health probe rewritten to 127.0.0.1
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm SrcIpAddr is genuinely external and not an internal monitor NAT'd to a public address; review HTTP status/size of the response. 2) Contain: set explicit trustedHosts in config/general.php, upgrade craftcms/cms to >=4.17.9 / >=5.9.15, and block the source IP. 3) Hunt: check for outbound fetches from php-fpm/nginx to internal or metadata IPs and for any /admin/actions/plugins/install-plugin POSTs in the following minutes.
ImWebSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(24h)
| where Url has "resource-js"
| extend HostHeader = tolower(tostring(HttpHostName))
| where HostHeader has_any ("localhost","127.0.0.1","[::1]","::1","0.0.0.0")
| where not(ipv4_is_private(SrcIpAddr)) and SrcIpAddr !in ("::1","127.0.0.1")
| project TimeGenerated, SrcIpAddr, HttpHostName, Url, HttpMethod, HttpStatusCode, UserAgent = HttpRequestHeaderUserAgent, DstHostname
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Blind SSRF: Craft web-server process fetching internal/cloud-metadata hosts AICommand & Control Medium
Detects the SSRF half of CVE-2026-55791 — Craft's Guzzle client (running under php-fpm/nginx/httpd) making outbound connections to RFC1918, link-local, or the 169.254.169.254 cloud-metadata endpoint after $baseUrl poisoning. Article-specific: the vuln forces this Guzzle fetch with ALLOW_REDIRECTS=true. Rationale: Targets the article's stated server-side fetch (Craft::createGuzzleClient()->get($url)) by pivoting on the web-server process making unexpected internal/metadata connections; the 169.254.169.254 focus is corroborated by Craft's related metadata-SSRF CVE-2026-27129, and DB ports are excluded to suppress routine backend traffic. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/composer/craftcms/cms/CVE-2026-55791/ • https://advisories.gitlab.com/pkg/composer/craftcms/cms/CVE-2026-27129/
ATT&CKT1090 T1190
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • php-fpm calling an internal microservice/API on an allowed private host (8080/9200)
  • nginx reverse-proxying to an internal app server
  • Legitimate use of an internal package mirror or Composer proxy
  • App-level health checks hitting internal services
  • Sidecar/service-mesh proxies on private IPs
Suppression filter
| where not (RemoteIP startswith "10." and RemotePort == 9200)  // example: drop known internal Elasticsearch; extend with allowed internal services after baselining
Response runbook
1) Triage: inspect the RemoteIP/RemoteUrl and the InitiatingProcessId — was 169.254.169.254 or an internal admin port hit, and is there a matching resource-js request in web logs? 2) Contain: rotate any cloud IAM credentials reachable via metadata if 169.254.169.254 was contacted, set explicit trustedHosts, and patch craftcms/cms. 3) Hunt: review web logs for resource-js Host poisoning from the originating window and for outbound connections to attacker-owned public IPs reflecting fetched JS.
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(24h)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName has_any ("php-fpm","php-cgi","httpd","apache2","nginx","w3wp.exe")
| where RemoteIP == "169.254.169.254"   // cloud metadata — classic blind-SSRF target (cf. CVE-2026-27129)
     or (RemoteIPType in ("Private","LinkLocal") and RemotePort in (80,443,8080,8000,8443,9200,2375,6379))
| where RemotePort !in (3306,5432,1433,11211)   // strip routine DB / memcache backend traffic
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessId, RemoteIP, RemotePort, RemoteUrl, InitiatingProcessAccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
ASCDeviceEvents
| where TimeGenerated > ago(24h)

Craft CMS 1-click RCE: plugin-install POST riding a poisoned resource-js JS load AIActions on Objectives High
Correlates the cache-poisoning payoff of CVE-2026-55791 — an admin browser that loaded the resource-js response as application/javascript then, within minutes from the same client, POSTs to /admin/actions/plugins/install-plugin (session riding using window.Craft.csrfTokenValue). This is the RCE step, not a generic CSRF check. Rationale: Uses the two concrete article URIs ('resource-js' returning application/javascript, and 'actions/plugins/install-plugin' POST) plus the same-client temporal link the cache-poisoning-to-session-riding chain requires; plugin install is a rare CP action so the correlation is high-fidelity and low-FP. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/composer/craftcms/cms/CVE-2026-55791/ • https://advisories.gitlab.com/composer/craftcms/cms/GHSA-95wr-3f2v-v2wh/
ATT&CKT1185 T1059.007
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Admin legitimately installing a plugin shortly after a normal Control-Panel page load
  • Automated CI/deploy tooling that installs plugins via the CP from a fixed IP
  • Penetration test reproducing the chain
  • Admin behind same NAT as the resource-js request during routine work
  • Plugin Store updates triggering install-plugin during a CP session
Suppression filter
| where UserAgent !has "CraftDeployBot"  // exclude known CI deploy automation user-agent after confirming it
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm whether the resource-js response served to that client was attacker-controlled (external Host header, unexpected size/hash) versus the legitimate cached asset; identify the admin account tied to the session. 2) Contain: disable/inspect the installed plugin, force-logout and rotate the admin session + CSRF, set explicit trustedHosts, and patch to >=4.17.9 / >=5.9.15. 3) Hunt: review the file system for newly written plugin/PHP files and the web-server process for child processes or outbound C2 following the install.
let lookback = 24h;
let window = 10m;
let resourcejs = ImWebSession
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(lookback)
    | where Url has "resource-js"
    | where HttpResponseHeaderContentType has "javascript"
    | project JsTime = TimeGenerated, SrcIpAddr, JsUrl = Url, JsHost = HttpHostName;
ImWebSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(lookback)
| where HttpMethod == "POST"
| where Url has "actions/plugins/install-plugin"
| join kind=inner resourcejs on SrcIpAddr
| where TimeGenerated between (JsTime .. JsTime + window)   // install POST within 10m of loading poisoned JS
| project InstallTime = TimeGenerated, SrcIpAddr, JsTime, JsHost, JsUrl, InstallUrl = Url, HttpStatusCode, UserAgent = HttpRequestHeaderUserAgent
| order by InstallTime desc

[GHSA / CRITICAL] GHSA-wfqx-gjrf-g28r: Crossplane: Signature verification TOCTOU allows installing unverified package content via mutable tag

GitHub Security Advisories
2026-06-19 2 use cases 0 techniques 1 kill-chain phase detected

Crossplane: Signature verification TOCTOU allows installing unverified package content via mutable tag

Click any ATT&CK pill below to open it on the Matrix

Actions on Objectives
Likely
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code.
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Crossplane package installed by mutable tag instead of immutable digest · [LLM] Crossplane ImageConfig signature-verification policy deleted or weakened
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Crossplane package installed by mutable tag instead of immutable digest AIInstallation High
Hunts Kubernetes API audit logs for pkg.crossplane.io Provider/Configuration/Function installs whose spec.package references a version tag rather than an @sha256 digest. This is the exact precondition GHSA-wfqx-gjrf-g28r requires: when a package is pulled by tag, a malicious/compromised OCI registry can serve a correctly-signed image for cosign verification and then swap in unsigned content for the actual pull. Rationale: Built directly on the advisory's stated exploit precondition #2 ('Install packages using tag references rather than digests'). The detection keys on the real Crossplane API (pkg.crossplane.io Provider/Configuration/Function, spec.package) and the digest sentinel '@sha256:' that the fix mandates; absence of the digest is exactly what makes an install TOCTOU-exposed. Corroborated against the GHSA, the GitLab advisory mirror, and Crossplane's ImageConfig/package docs. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-wfqx-gjrf-g28r • https://advisories.gitlab.com/golang/github.com/crossplane/crossplane/v2/GHSA-wfqx-gjrf-g28r/ • https://docs.crossplane.io/latest/packages/image-configs/
ATT&CKT1195.002
Data sources
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate GitOps/Argo/Flux pipelines that pin Crossplane packages to a semantic-version tag (e.g. provider-aws:v1.20.0) rather than a digest — common and benign in many shops
  • Platform-team installs from the org's own trusted internal registry where tag mutability is acceptable
  • Dev/sandbox clusters where engineers iterate on packages by floating tag
  • Initial cluster bootstrap installing first-party Upbound/Crossplane marketplace packages by tag
  • Renovate/Dependabot-style bots bumping a package tag during routine upgrades
Suppression filter
| where not (PackageRef startswith "xpkg.upbound.io/upbound/" or PackageRef startswith "xpkg.crossplane.io/")   // keep third-party / org-uncontrolled registries (advisory precondition #3), drop trusted first-party marketplace
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the offending Provider/Configuration object and its spec.package. Confirm whether the registry host is one the org controls (advisory precondition #3) and whether an ImageConfig with cosign verification matches that image. A tag-pinned package from a third-party registry under cosign verification is the high-risk case. 2) Contain: re-pin the package to the resolved immutable digest (spec.package: repo@sha256:...) and ensure Crossplane is on a fixed build (v2.3.3 / v2.2.3 or later); for vulnerable builds, prefer digest references org-wide until upgraded. 3) Hunt: inventory all pkg.crossplane.io resources still on tags, review the installed package revisions' Verified condition for skipped/incomplete status, and check whether the registry served differing digests for verify vs pull around the install time.
AKSAudit
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where Verb in ("create", "update", "patch")
| extend ObjRef = todynamic(ObjectRef)
| where tostring(ObjRef.apiGroup) == "pkg.crossplane.io"
| where tostring(ObjRef.resource) in ("providers", "configurations", "functions")
| extend ReqObj = todynamic(RequestObject)
| extend PackageRef = tostring(ReqObj.spec.package)
| where isnotempty(PackageRef) and PackageRef !contains "@sha256:"   // not digest-pinned => mutable tag => TOCTOU-exposed
| extend Actor = tostring(todynamic(User).username)
| project TimeGenerated, Actor, Verb, Kind = tostring(ObjRef.resource), ObjectName = tostring(ObjRef.name), PackageRef, UserAgent
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Crossplane Package Installed By Mutable Tag Without Digest Pin
id: 7a3c1e8b-2f4d-4c9a-b6e1-9d2f5a1c3e47
description: Detects creation/update of a Crossplane Provider, Configuration or Function whose spec.package uses a mutable tag rather than an immutable @sha256 digest, the precondition for the GHSA-wfqx-gjrf-g28r signature-verification TOCTOU.
references:
  - https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-wfqx-gjrf-g28r
  - https://docs.crossplane.io/latest/packages/image-configs/
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/06/21
tags:
  - attack.t1195.002
logsource:
  product: kubernetes
  service: audit
detection:
  selection:
    verb:
      - create
      - update
      - patch
    objectRef.apiGroup: 'pkg.crossplane.io'
    objectRef.resource:
      - 'providers'
      - 'configurations'
      - 'functions'
  filter_digest:
    requestObject.spec.package|contains: '@sha256:'
  condition: selection and not filter_digest
level: low
source:kubernetes.audit @objectRef.apiGroup:pkg.crossplane.io @objectRef.resource:(providers OR configurations OR functions) @verb:(create OR update OR patch) -@requestObject.spec.package:*sha256*
CLOUD
sourcetype="kube:apiserver:audit" objectRef.apiGroup="pkg.crossplane.io" (objectRef.resource="providers" OR objectRef.resource="configurations" OR objectRef.resource="functions") (verb="create" OR verb="update" OR verb="patch")
| rename requestObject.spec.package as package_ref, objectRef.name as object_name, objectRef.resource as kind, user.username as username
| where isnotnull(package_ref) AND NOT match(package_ref, "@sha256:")
| stats min(_time) as firstTime, max(_time) as lastTime, values(username) as username, values(sourceIPs{}) as src_ips by package_ref, kind, object_name
| convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
| sort - lastTime
Crossplane ImageConfig signature-verification policy deleted or weakened AIInstallation Medium
Alerts on delete/update/patch operations against imageconfigs.pkg.crossplane.io in the Kubernetes audit stream. Crossplane enforces cosign signature verification only for images matched by an ImageConfig (spec.verification.cosign); removing or editing that resource silently disables verification, the defense the GHSA-wfqx-gjrf-g28r TOCTOU already partially undermines. Surfaces whether the post-change object still carries a verification block. Rationale: Grounded in the advisory's statement that 'package signature verification [is] configured via the ImageConfig mechanism' and Crossplane docs confirming the real resource (imageconfigs.pkg.crossplane.io, spec.verification.cosign). Detects the defense-evasion precursor — tearing down or weakening the cosign policy — using only verifiable API/resource names (no skipSignatureVerification field exists in the schema, so that reviewer suggestion was dropped as ungrounded). Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-wfqx-gjrf-g28r • https://docs.crossplane.io/latest/packages/image-configs/ • https://docs.upbound.io/providers/signature-verification
ATT&CKT1562.001
Data sources
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Platform engineers legitimately rotating cosign authorities/keys inside an ImageConfig during key rollover
  • GitOps reconciliation re-applying an unchanged ImageConfig (server-side apply 'update' with no real diff)
  • Decommissioning an ImageConfig for a registry the org no longer uses
  • Migrating verification config between ImageConfig objects (delete old + create new)
  • Cert-manager or admission-controller status writes touching the resource
Suppression filter
| where Verb in ("delete", "deletecollection") or VerificationSpecPresent == false   // alert on removal/teardown of verification; suppress edits that retain a verification block
Response runbook
1) Triage: diff the ImageConfig before/after the operation — confirm whether spec.verification.cosign authorities were removed, loosened, or the whole object deleted, and identify the actor (human vs service account) and source IP. A delete or removal of the verification block by a non-platform identity is high-confidence malicious. 2) Contain: restore the ImageConfig from source-of-truth/GitOps, and pause new package installs (or require digest pins) until verification is re-established; rotate credentials of the actor if unauthorized. 3) Hunt: list any package revisions installed while the ImageConfig was absent/weakened and check their Verified condition; cross-reference with the mutable-tag install detection to find packages that may have been pulled without enforced verification.
AKSAudit
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| extend ObjRef = todynamic(ObjectRef)
| where tostring(ObjRef.apiGroup) == "pkg.crossplane.io"
| where tostring(ObjRef.resource) == "imageconfigs"
| where Verb in ("delete", "deletecollection", "update", "patch")
| extend ReqObj = todynamic(RequestObject)
| extend VerificationSpecPresent = isnotempty(tostring(ReqObj.spec.verification))
| extend Actor = tostring(todynamic(User).username)
| project TimeGenerated, Actor, Verb, ImageConfigName = tostring(ObjRef.name), UserAgent, VerificationSpecPresent
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Crossplane ImageConfig Signature Verification Deleted Or Modified
id: b8f2d6a4-1c3e-4f7b-9a2d-6e4c8b1f0a52
description: Detects delete or modify operations against Crossplane ImageConfig resources, which configure cosign package signature verification. Removing or weakening an ImageConfig disables verification for matching package images (GHSA-wfqx-gjrf-g28r).
references:
  - https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-wfqx-gjrf-g28r
  - https://docs.crossplane.io/latest/packages/image-configs/
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/06/21
tags:
  - attack.t1562.001
logsource:
  product: kubernetes
  service: audit
detection:
  selection:
    objectRef.apiGroup: 'pkg.crossplane.io'
    objectRef.resource: 'imageconfigs'
    verb:
      - delete
      - deletecollection
      - update
      - patch
  condition: selection
level: medium
source:kubernetes.audit @objectRef.apiGroup:pkg.crossplane.io @objectRef.resource:imageconfigs @verb:(delete OR deletecollection OR update OR patch)
CLOUD
sourcetype="kube:apiserver:audit" objectRef.apiGroup="pkg.crossplane.io" objectRef.resource="imageconfigs" (verb="delete" OR verb="deletecollection" OR verb="update" OR verb="patch")
| rename objectRef.name as imageconfig_name, user.username as username, verb as action, userAgent as user_agent
| spath input=_raw path=requestObject.spec.verification output=verification_spec
| eval verification_present=if(isnull(verification_spec) OR verification_spec="", "no", "yes")
| table _time, username, action, imageconfig_name, user_agent, verification_present, sourceIPs{}
| sort - _time
